height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}

Here, the deletion block is

14

15

in the first column, and the insertion blocks are

5

6

7

in the third column, and

4

in the fourth column. Notice that outside of these blocks,
P
and
P^{\prime}
are the same, and each block only changes by adding a number and removing a number from the block.

We now view the effect of reverse insertion
as changing the block in each column
of
P
to obtain
P^{\prime}
.
We set
P^{(C-1)}=P
.
For
c\geq C
, let
P^{(c)}

be the tableau obtained from
P^{c-1}

by changing the block in column
c
if it exists,
or
P^{(c)}=P^{(c-1)}
if
there is no block in column
c
.
Then
P^{\prime}=P^{(m)}
for some
m
large enough.
To prove Theorem
6.8
,
we just need to understand how
P^{(c)}

differs from
P^{(c-1)}
.
First, we study the effect
of changing an insertion block.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose the block in column
c

is an insertion block and
let
x
be the value inserted
to this block.
Then
c>1
and

|(P_{c})_{<x}|=|(P^{\prime}_{c-1})_{<x}|
.

Proof.

Let
(r,c)
be the bottom-most cell of this insertion block, so
P(r,c)=x

and
P^{\prime}(r,c)>x
.
Moreover,
P^{\prime}(r,c-1)=P(r,c-1)<x
.
We know
x
was inserted into
(r,c)
through a direct or indirect replacement, so
P(r+1,c^{\prime})=x
for some
c^{\prime}
.
We consider two cases:

•

Suppose the cell
(r+1,c)

does not exist in
P
,
then trivially
c^{\prime}<c

and
|(P_{c})_{<x}|=r
.
If
(r+1,c-1)
does not exist in
P
either,
then
|(P_{c-1})_{<x}|=r
.
Otherwise,

P^{\prime}(r+1,c-1)\geq P(r+1,c-1)\geq x
,
so
|(P^{\prime}_{c-1})_{<x}|=r
.

•

Suppose the cell
(r+1,c)

exists in
P
.
By
P^{\prime}
is increasing,

x<P^{\prime}(r+1,c)=P(r+1,c)
,
so
|(P_{c})_{<x}|=r
.
Then
c^{\prime}<c
and

x\leq P^{\prime}(r+1,c-1)
,
so
|(P^{\prime}_{c-1})_{<x}|=r
. ∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose the block in column
c

is an insertion block and
let
x
be the value inserted
to this block. Then the insertion block removes the number
\min\{t\in P_{c}:x\sqcup P_{c}-t\preceq P_{c+1}\}
.

Proof.

Let
z
be the number removed by the insertion block
in column
c
.
We have
P^{\prime}_{c}=x\sqcup P_{c}-z

and
P^{\prime}_{c}\preceq P_{c+1}
.
Assume
P(r,c)=z
.
By definition of the insertion block, the algorithm cannot perform another direct replacement step in column
c
in row
r-1
. Thus, either
r=1
or
z\geq P_{c+1}(r-1)
. In either case, for any
i\in[r-1]
, we cannot have
x\sqcup P_{c}-P_{c}(i)\preceq P_{c+1}
.
Our claim is therefore proved.
∎

We now understand the effect of changing an insertion block.

Lemma 6.12
.

Suppose the block in column
c

is an insertion block,
then
K_{-}(P^{(c)})=K_{-}(P^{(c-1)})
.

Proof.

Say the insertion block gains
x
and loses
y
,
so
P^{(c)}_{c}=x\sqcup P^{(c-1)}_{c}-y
.
By Corollary
5.10
,
K_{-}(P^{(c-1)})_{i}=P^{(c-1)}_{1,i}
and
K_{-}(P^{(c)})_{i}=P^{(c)}_{1,i}
. Since
P^{(c-1)}
and
P^{(c)}
only differ in column
c
,
we know
K_{-}(P^{(c-1)})_{i}=K_{-}(P^{(c)})_{i}

if
i<c
.

By Lemma
6.10
,

c>1
.
Notice that
P^{(c-1)}_{c-1}=P^{(c)}_{c-1}=P^{\prime}_{c-1}
,

P^{(c-1)}_{c+1}=P^{(c)}_{c+1}=P_{c+1}
,

P^{(c-1)}_{c}=P_{c}
,
and
P^{(c)}_{c}=P^{\prime}_{c}
.
We set
U=P^{\prime}_{c-1}
,

T=P_{c}
and
S=P_{c+1}
.
By Lemma
6.10

and Lemma
6.11
,

U,T,S
and
x,y
satisfy
the conditions of
Lemma
4.16
.
Thus,

P^{\prime}_{c-1}\lhd P_{c}=P^{\prime}_{c-1}\lhd P^{\prime}_{c}

and

P^{\prime}_{c-1}\lhd P_{c}\lhd S^{\prime}=P^{\prime}_{c-1}\lhd P^{\prime}_{c}\lhd S^{\prime}.

for any
S^{\prime}\subseteq P_{c+1}
.
The first equation implies

P^{\prime}_{1,c}=P_{1,c}
.
The second equation implies

P^{\prime}_{1,i}=P_{1,i}

if
i>c
.
∎

Next, we study
the deletion block.

Lemma 6.13
.

Suppose the block
in column
c
is a deletion block.
Then this block loses
\min\{t\in P_{c}:P_{c}-t\preceq P_{c+1}\}
.

Proof.

By a similar proof of Lemma
6.11
.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

If a deletion block occurs in column
c
, then
K_{-}(P)

and
K_{-}(P^{(c)})
agree except in column
c
,
where

(6.1)

\displaystyle K_{-}(P^{(c)})_{c}=K_{-}(P)_{c}-\min(K_{-}(P)_{c}\setminus K_{-}(P)_{c+1})

Proof.

Recall that
P^{(c)}
is obtained from
P

by changing the deletion block.
Assume the deletion block loses
y
, so
P^{(c)}_{c}=P_{c}-y
.
By Lemma
6.13
,
y=\min\{t\in P_{c}:P_{c}-t\preceq P_{c+1}\}
.

By Corollary
5.10
,
column
i
of
K_{-}(P)
and

K_{-}(P^{(c)})
are
P_{1,i}

and
P^{(c)}_{1,i}
respectively.
They clearly agree if
i<c
.
Suppose
i>c
.
To show
P_{1,i}=P^{(c)}_{1,i}
,
it is enough to check
P_{c,i}=P^{(c)}_{1,i}
,
which is

P_{c}\lhd P_{c+1,i}=(P_{c}-y)\lhd P_{c+1,i}
.
Since
P_{c+1,i}\subseteq P_{c+1}
,
this equation is implied by
the third statement in Lemma
4.17
.

Finally, we show

P^{(c)}_{i,c}=P_{i,c}-\min(P_{i,c}\setminus P_{i,c+1})

for all
i\in[c]
by induction on
i
from
i=c
to
i=1
.
Setting
i=1
would imply (
6.1
).
For the base case
i=c
,
we know
P_{c,c}^{(c)}=P_{c,c}-y
.
It remains to check
y
is the smallest
number in
P_{c}
that is not in
P_{c,c+1}
.
This is done by the first two statements in
4.17
.
For the inductive step, assume
i<c
. We have

P^{(c)}_{i,c}=P_{i}\triangleleft P^{(c)}_{i+1,c}=P_{i}\triangleleft(P_{i+1,c}-\min(P_{i+1,c}\setminus P_{i+1,c+1}))=P_{i,c}-\min(P_{i,c}\setminus P_{i,c+1}),

where the last
two steps are given by the inductive hypothesis and Lemma
4.15
respectively.
∎

Now we have understood how
K_{-}(P^{(c)})

differs from
K_{-}(P^{(c-1)})
.

Proof of Theorem
6.8
.

Consider the sequence of increasing tableaux:

P=P^{(C-1)},P^{(C)},P^{(C+1)},\cdots,P^{(m-1)},P^{(m)}=P^{\prime},

If
\alpha=0
,
then there is no deletion block.
By Lemma
6.12
,

K_{-}(P)=K_{-}(P^{(C)})=\cdots=K_{-}(P^{\prime})
.
If
\alpha=1
,
then the block in column
c
is a
deletion block and all other blocks are insertion blocks.
By Lemma
6.12
,

K_{-}(P^{(C)})=K_{-}(P^{(C+1)})=\cdots=K_{-}(P^{\prime})
.
Then by Lemma
6.14
,

K_{-}(P^{\prime})
agrees with
K_{-}(P)
except in column
c

where
K_{-}(P^{\prime})_{c}=K_{-}(P)_{c}-\min(K_{-}(P)_{c}\setminus K_{-}(P)_{c+1})
.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.1

In this section, we prove Theorem
3.1

using similar idea as
[
16
, Theorem 4.2]
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Since
\Psi
is a bijection from
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{C}
, it suffices to show that for any
(P,Q)\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
,
\Psi(P,Q)\in\mathcal{C}^{b}
and for any
(a,i)\in\mathcal{C}^{b}
,
\Psi^{-1}(a,i)\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
.

Take
(P,Q)\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
where
Q
has at least one cell. Let
i_{1}=\min(Q)
. Let
(r,c)
be the rightmost cell in
Q
with
i_{1}\in Q(r,c)
. Let
\alpha=1
if
i_{1}
is the only number in
Q(r,c)
and
\alpha=0
otherwise.
Let
(P^{\prime},a_{1})
be the output of reverse insertion on
(P,(r,c),\alpha)
.
Let
Q^{\prime}
be the tableau obtained from
Q

by removing
i_{1}
from
Q(r,c)
,
and also remove the cell
(r,c)
if
\alpha=1
.
Due to the recursive definition of
\Psi
,

\Psi(P,Q)=(a_{1}\circ a^{\prime},i_{1}\circ i^{\prime})

where
(a^{\prime},i^{\prime})=\Psi(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
.
Notice that
(a,i)\in\mathcal{C}^{b}

is equivalent to
a_{1}\leq i_{1}

and
(a^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{b}
.
Inductively, we may assume
(a^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{b}
if and only if
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
.
Thus, it suffices to show the following two statements:

•

If
(P,Q)\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
,
then
a_{1}\geq i_{1}
and
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
.

•

If
a_{1}\geq i_{1}
and
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
,
then
(P,Q)\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
.

We start with the first statement.
To see
a_{1}\geq i_{1}
, observe that
i_{1}=\min(Q)
implies
i_{1}\leq\min(L(Q))
. By the definition of
K_{-}(\cdot)
on RSSYT,
\min(L(Q))\leq\min(K_{-}(L(Q)))
, which in turn is at most
\min(K_{-}(P))\leq\min(P)\leq a_{1}
.
Next we show
(P^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in\mathcal{T}^{b}
. If
\alpha=0
, then Theorem
6.7
and Theorem
6.8
yield

K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))=K_{-}(L(Q))\leq K_{-}(P)=K_{-}(P^{\prime})
.
Now suppose
\alpha=1
.
By Theorem
6.8
the only column where
K_{-}(P)
and
K_{-}(P^{\prime})
differ is column
c
and
(K_{-}(P^{\prime}))_{c}=(K_{-}(P))_{c}-x
, where
x=\min((K_{-}(P))_{c}\setminus(K_{-}(P))_{c+1})
.
By Theorem
6.7
,
the only column where
K_{-}(L(Q))
and
K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))
differ is column
c
and
(K_{-}(Q^{\prime}))_{c}=(K_{-}(Q))_{c}-y
for some
y
.
It remains to check for each
j\in[|K_{-}(P^{\prime})_{c}|]
,
K_{-}(P^{\prime})_{c}(j)\geq K_{-}(Q^{\prime})_{c}(j)
.

For
j
such that
(K_{-}(P))_{c}(j)\geq x
,

(K_{-}(P^{\prime}))_{c}(j)=(K_{-}(P))_{c}(j+1)\geq(K_{-}(L(Q)))_{c}(j+1)\geq(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c}(j).

For
j
such that
(K_{-}(P))(j)<x
,

(K_{-}(P^{\prime}))_{c}(j)=(K_{-}(P^{\prime}))_{c+1}(j)\geq(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c+1}(j)\geq(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c}(j).

Therefore, for all
j
,
(K_{-}(P^{\prime}))_{c}(j)\geq(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c}(j)
, which shows that
K_{-}(P^{\prime})\geq K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))
.

We now show the second statement.
If
\alpha=0
, then Theorem
6.7
and Theorem
6.8
yield

K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))=K_{-}(L(Q))\leq K_{-}(P)=K_{-}(P^{\prime})
.
Otherwise,
\alpha=1
. By Theorem
6.8
, the only column where
K_{-}(P)
and
K_{-}(P^{\prime})
differ is column
c
and
(K_{-}(P))_{c}=(K_{-}(P^{\prime}))_{c}\sqcup x
for some
x
. By Theorem
6.7
, the only column where
K_{-}(L(Q))
and
K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))
differ is column
c
, and
K_{-}(L(Q))_{c}=K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))_{c}\sqcup y
,
where

y=\begin{cases}i_{1}&\text{if $c=1$,}\\
\min(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))_{c-1}-K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime}))_{c})&\text{if $c>1$.}\end{cases}

It remains to check for each
j\in[|K_{-}(P)_{c}|]
,
K_{-}(P)_{c}(j)\geq K_{-}(Q)_{c}(j)
.
For
j
such that
(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c}(j)>y
,

(K_{-}(P))_{c}(j)\geq(K_{-}(P^{\prime}))_{c}(j-1)\geq(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c}(j-1)=(K_{-}(L(Q)))_{c}(j).

Now assume
(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c}(j)\leq y
.
If
c=1
,
y=i_{1}
is the smallest
number in
i
, so it is the smallest
number in
Q
.
We know
(K_{-}(L(Q^{\prime})))_{c}(j)=y

and
y\leq\min(a)=\min(P)\leq(K_{-}(P))_{c}(j)
.
If
c>1
, we have

K_{-}(P)_{c}(j)\geq K_{-}(P)_{c-1}(j)\geq K_{-}(L(Q))_{c-1}(j)=K_{-}(L(Q))_{c}(j)

This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we mention a by-product of Theorem
3.1
:
the Grothendieck-to-Lascoux expansion.
It was conjectured by
[
14
]

and proved in
[
16
]
.

Corollary 6.15
.

For a permutation
w\in S_{n}
,

\mathfrak{G}^{(\beta)}_{w}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathrm{Inc}\\
{\sf word}(P)=w^{-1}\end{subarray}}\beta^{|\mathrm{wt}(K_{-}(P))|-\ell(w)}\mathfrak{L}^{(\beta)}_{\mathrm{wt}(K_{-}(P))}.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
3.1
, Equation
2.4
, and Equation
2.1
.
∎

7.
